Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

      AND (Filesystem.file_name IN ("node.js"))
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

[LLM] Vulnerable react-server-dom-* package versions (CVE-2025-55182) in workload inventory Reconnaissance High
Identifies hosts running react-server-dom-webpack / react-server-dom-parcel / react-server-dom-turbopack at versions 19.0.0, 19.1.0, 19.1.1, or 19.2.0 — the exact builds Snyk and Meta name as exploitable for unauthenticated RCE via the RSC Flight protocol. Used pre-patch to scope exposure and post-patch to verify completion. Rationale: Anchors on the three exact package names (react-server-dom-webpack/parcel/turbopack) and four exact vulnerable versions Snyk enumerates, plus CveId CVE-2025-55182. Joined with DeviceInfo.IsInternetFacing so internet-exposed Next.js workloads can be prioritised, matching Snyk's stated 39% cloud-exposure figure. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/security-advisory-critical-rce-vulnerabilities-react-server-components/ • https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories • https://github.com/vercel/next.js/security/advisories
ATT&CKT1190

   or (SoftwareName =~ "next" and SoftwareVersion matches regex @"^(15\.|16\.)")

| order by IsInternetFacing desc, Timestamp desc

[LLM] Node.js process spawning interactive shell — suspected post-exploit RCE on Next.js / RSC server Exploitation High
Hunts for node.exe / node spawning cmd.exe, powershell.exe, pwsh.exe, sh, or bash on hosts running Next.js or any framework embedding react-server-dom-*. Production Node web servers virtually never need a child shell; this is the highest-fidelity behavioural signature of CVE-2025-55182 exploitation reaching command execution. Rationale: Snyk states the RCE 'occurs before any routing logic or auth gates' on a single crafted HTTP request to an RSC endpoint, so the post-exploit foothold lives inside the Node.js process serving that endpoint. A node-parented shell is the cleanest article-specific behavioural pivot. Build-time exclusions (`next build`/`next dev`, npm/yarn/pnpm parents) suppress the only realistic FP source. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/security-advisory-critical-rce-vulnerabilities-react-server-components/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1059.003 T1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","sh","bash","dash","zsh","ash")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where AccountName !in~ ("system","local service","network service")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "next build"
      and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "next dev"
      and InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("npm.exe","npm","yarn.exe","yarn","pnpm.exe","pnpm","npm-cli.js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd   = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          GrandparentImage = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          ChildImage  = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd    = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(^|[\\/])node(\.exe)?$"
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(^|[\\/])(cmd\.exe|powershell\.exe|pwsh\.exe|sh|bash|dash|zsh|ash)$"
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessCommandLine !has "next build" and ParentProcessCommandLine !has "next dev"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Node.js Process Spawning Interactive Shell - Suspected RSC RCE (CVE-2025-55182)
id: 7b3f1e2a-5c8d-4a91-9f3e-1d2c8f4b6e0a
description: Detects node.exe / node spawning cmd.exe, powershell.exe, pwsh.exe, sh, bash, dash, or zsh. Production Next.js / React Server Components workloads should not require a child shell; this pattern is the highest-fidelity behavioural indicator of post-exploitation following CVE-2025-55182 (unauthenticated RCE in react-server-dom-* Flight deserialiser).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/security-advisory-critical-rce-vulnerabilities-react-server-components/
  - https://github.com/facebook/react/security/advisories
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/12/03
tags:
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.001
  - attack.t1059.003
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.t1190
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '/node'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
      - '/zsh'
      - '/ash'
  filter_build:
    ParentCommandLine|contains:
      - 'next build'
      - 'next dev'
  filter_pkg_manager:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\yarn.exe'
      - '\pnpm.exe'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child and not (filter_build or filter_pkg_manager)
falsepositives:
  - Next.js build pipelines invoking shell helpers via npm scripts
  - Developer machines running `next dev` with custom prebuild hooks
level: high
(source:windows.sysmon OR source:linux.auditd) @EventID:1 (@ParentImage:(*\node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE) OR @ParentImage:*/node OR @process.parent.executable.name:node) (@Image:(*\cmd.exe OR *\CMD.EXE OR *\powershell.exe OR *\PowerShell.exe OR *\POWERSHELL.EXE OR *\pwsh.exe OR *\PWSH.EXE) OR @Image:(/bin/sh OR /bin/bash OR /usr/bin/sh OR /usr/bin/bash OR /bin/dash OR /bin/zsh) OR @process.executable.name:(sh OR bash OR dash OR zsh)) NOT @ParentCommandLine:(*"next build"* OR *"next dev"*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^node(\.exe)?$/i ImageFileName=/(\\|\/)(cmd\.exe|powershell\.exe|pwsh\.exe|sh|bash|dash|zsh|ash)$/i
| NOT (ParentCommandLine=/next\s+(build|dev)/i)
| NOT (ParentCommandLine=/(npm|yarn|pnpm)\s+(run|exec)/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

[LLM] Anomalous POST to Next.js Server Action / RSC endpoint with 5xx error clustering Exploitation Medium
Targets Snyk's two explicit runtime indicators: 'requests to RSC routes containing malformed or unexpected RSC Flight frames' and 'sudden increases in server function invocation errors'. Surfaces POST traffic carrying the `Next-Action` header or `?_rsc=` / `RSC=1` markers that returns clusters of 5xx responses from a single source IP — the wire-level shape of a crafted-payload probe against the unsafe Flight deserialiser. Rationale: Snyk's own runtime detection guidance names exactly these two signals: malformed RSC Flight frames on RSC routes AND sudden spikes in server function invocation errors. Anchored on the Next.js-specific wire markers `/_next/`, `?_rsc=`, `RSC=1`, and the `Next-Action` request header that the App Router emits for server actions — these are public Next.js conventions, not inferred. 5xx clustering by source IP within a 5m bucket lifts the signal above ordinary error noise (P95 in healthy Next.js is <2 5xx per source IP per 5m). Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/security-advisory-critical-rce-vulnerabilities-react-server-components/ • https://nextjs.org/docs/app/building-your-application/data-fetching/server-actions-and-mutations • https://github.com/vercel/next.js/security/advisories
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb
// Defender XDR has limited HTTP-server-log telemetry; this pivots on outbound connections initiated by the Node.js process serving RSC after a burst of inbound 5xx (use UC2/UC3-network combo if HTTP logs unavailable).
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| where ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| summarize ConnCount = count(), DistinctRemotes = dcount(RemoteIP), Remotes = make_set(RemoteIP, 20), SampleCmd = any(InitiatingProcessCommandLine) by DeviceName, bin(Timestamp, 5m)
| where DistinctRemotes >= 3 or ConnCount > 20
| order by Timestamp desc
let WindowMin = 5m;
let ErrorThreshold = 10;
union isfuzzy=true
    (AppServiceHTTPLogs
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
        | where CsMethod == "POST"
        | where (CsUriStem startswith "/_next/" or CsUriQuery has "_rsc=" or CsUriQuery has "RSC=1")
        | where ScStatus >= 500 and ScStatus < 600
        | extend SrcIP = CIp, Host = CsHost, Uri = CsUriStem, Status = ScStatus, Method = CsMethod, UA = UserAgent),
    (W3CIISLog
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
        | where csMethod == "POST"
        | where (csUriStem startswith "/_next/" or csUriQuery has "_rsc=" or csUriQuery has "RSC=1")
        | where scStatus >= 500 and scStatus < 600
        | extend SrcIP = cIP, Host = sComputerName, Uri = csUriStem, Status = scStatus, Method = csMethod, UA = csUserAgent),
    (CommonSecurityLog
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
        | where RequestMethod == "POST"
        | where (RequestURL contains "/_next/" or RequestURL contains "_rsc=" or RequestURL contains "RSC=1" or RequestContext has "Next-Action")
        | where toint(EventOutcome) >= 500 and toint(EventOutcome) < 600
        | extend SrcIP = SourceIP, Host = DestinationHostName, Uri = RequestURL, Status = toint(EventOutcome), Method = RequestMethod, UA = RequestClientApplication)
| summarize ErrorCount = count(), DistinctUris = dcount(Uri), SampleUris = make_set(Uri, 10), SampleUA = any(UA) by SrcIP, Host, bin(TimeGenerated, WindowMin)
| where ErrorCount >= ErrorThreshold     // empirical — clean Next.js apps emit <2 5xx/5m per source IP
| order by ErrorCount desc

union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    - None on a true-positive hash match
level: critical

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-26828")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-26828")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

[LLM] MuddyWater Fooder loader (OsUpdater.exe) execution from Downloads Installation High
Detects execution of the Fooder reflective loader dropped as OsUpdater.exe in the user's Downloads folder, including known ESET-published SHA-1 hashes. Fooder is the dropper that reflectively loads the MuddyViper backdoor; it disguises itself as the classic Snake game with custom Sleep-API delay logic. Rationale: Targets ESET-published SHA-1 hashes plus the article-specific OsUpdater.exe drop path under %USERPROFILE%\Downloads. Multiple Fooder variants share this exact filename and Downloads landing location — the SHA-1 IOC list is corroborated across ESET, SecurityAffairs and DarkReading write-ups. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/muddywater-snakes-riverbank/ • https://securityaffairs.com/185244/apt/muddywater-strikes-israel-with-advanced-muddyviper-malware.html • https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/irans-muddywater-levels-up-muddyviper-backdoor
ATT&CKT1204.002 T1620 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName =~ "OsUpdater.exe" and FolderPath has @"\Downloads\")
   or SHA1 in~ (
     "76632910CF67697BF5D7285FAE38BFCF438EC082",
     "0657D0B0610618886DDD74C3D0A1D582CDD24863",
     "2939FD218E0145D730BD94AA1C76386A5259EACE",
     "1723D5EA7185D2E339FA9529D245DAA5D5C9A932",
     "8E21DE54638A79D8489C59D958B23FE22E90944A",
     "29CDA06701F9A9C0A6791775C3EB70F5B52BBEFF"
   )
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1,
          ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

          CommandLine, ParentProcessName, TokenElevationType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

[LLM] MuddyViper persistence via ManageOnDriveUpdater scheduled task or Startup folder hijack Installation High
Detects MuddyViper backdoor persistence in two complementary ways: creation of the 'ManageOnDriveUpdater' scheduled task and registry tampering against HKCU Explorer User Shell Folders\Startup to redirect the per-user Startup folder. Both are ESET-documented MuddyViper persistence techniques in the Snakes-by-the-riverbank campaign. Rationale: Hunts two ESET-published MuddyViper persistence pivots: the exact task name 'ManageOnDriveUpdater' (very low-FP literal string) and the unusual Startup-folder redirection via HKCU User Shell Folders — Windows never tampers with this value during legitimate flows, so any non-Explorer write is high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/muddywater-snakes-riverbank/ • https://securityaffairs.com/185244/apt/muddywater-strikes-israel-with-advanced-muddyviper-malware.html
ATT&CKT1053.005 T1547.001 T1112
Data sourcesEndpoint.Registry Endpoint.Processes
let SchedTaskHits =
    DeviceEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ActionType == "ScheduledTaskCreated"
    | where AdditionalFields has "ManageOnDriveUpdater" or FileName has "ManageOnDriveUpdater"
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, Signal = "SchedTask:ManageOnDriveUpdater",
              AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, AdditionalFields;
let StartupHijackHits =
    DeviceRegistryEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ActionType in ("RegistryValueSet","RegistryKeyCreated")
    | where RegistryKey has @"\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\User Shell Folders"
         or RegistryKey has @"\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\Shell Folders"
    | where RegistryValueName =~ "Startup"
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("explorer.exe","svchost.exe","msiexec.exe","setup.exe")
    | where not(RegistryValueData has_cs @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, Signal = "Reg:UserShellFolders.Startup",
              AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              AdditionalFields = strcat("key=", RegistryKey, " val=", RegistryValueName, " data=", RegistryValueData);
union SchedTaskHits, StartupHijackHits
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| order by Timestamp desc
let SchedTaskHits =
    SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where EventID == 4698
    | extend TaskNameRaw = tostring(EventData)
    | where TaskNameRaw has "ManageOnDriveUpdater"
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, Signal = "SchedTask:ManageOnDriveUpdater",
              Account = SubjectUserName, Detail = TaskNameRaw;
let StartupHijackHits =
    ImRegistryEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where RegistryKey has @"\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\User Shell Folders"
         or RegistryKey has @"\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\Shell Folders"
    | where RegistryValue =~ "Startup"
    | where not(RegistryValueData has_cs @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup")
    | where ActingProcessName !in~ ("explorer.exe","svchost.exe","msiexec.exe")
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer = DvcHostname, Signal = "Reg:UserShellFolders.Startup",
              Account = ActorUsername, Detail = strcat("key=", RegistryKey, " val=", RegistryValue, " data=", RegistryValueData);
union SchedTaskHits, StartupHijackHits
| where Account !endswith "$"
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:windows.security (@EventID:4698 (@EventData:*ManageOnDriveUpdater* OR @TaskName:*ManageOnDriveUpdater*)) OR (source:windows.sysmon @EventID:13 (@TargetObject:(*\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\User Shell Folders\Startup OR *\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Explorer\Shell Folders\Startup) OR @TargetObject:(*\software\microsoft\windows\currentversion\explorer\user shell folders\startup)) -@Image:(*\explorer.exe OR *\Explorer.exe OR *\EXPLORER.EXE OR *\svchost.exe OR *\Svchost.exe OR *\SVCHOST.EXE))
(#event_simpleName=AsepValueUpdate RegObjectName=/\\Explorer\\(User Shell Folders|Shell Folders)$/i RegValueName=/^Startup$/i NOT RegStringValue=/Microsoft\\Windows\\Start Menu\\Programs\\Startup$/i) OR (#event_simpleName=ScheduledTaskRegistered TaskName=/ManageOnDriveUpdater/i) OR (#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 CommandLine=/(schtasks|Register-ScheduledTask).*ManageOnDriveUpdater/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, RegObjectName, RegValueName, RegStringValue, TaskName], function=count())

[LLM] MuddyViper C2 fingerprint: 'A WinHTTP Example Program/1.0' UA + distinctive URI paths Command & Control High
Detects MuddyViper command-and-control traffic by joining its hard-coded WinHTTP sample User-Agent 'A WinHTTP Example Program/1.0' with the documented short URI tokens (/adad, /aq36, /mq65, /oi32, /dadw, /dadwqa, /rq13) used for command pull, browser-data upload, reverse-shell output and credential exfil. Also matches network connections to the known MuddyViper C2 domains. Rationale: Joins three ESET-confirmed C2 fingerprints — the sample User-Agent string left in MuddyViper, the seven short URI tokens for the backdoor's command and data verbs, and the C2 IP/domain set. Any one of these alone is high-fidelity; combining them gives durable coverage even if attackers rotate infrastructure. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/muddywater-snakes-riverbank/ • https://securityaffairs.com/185244/apt/muddywater-strikes-israel-with-advanced-muddyviper-malware.html • https://therecord.media/iran-linked-hackers-target-israel-egypt-phishing
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1132.001 T1041
Data sourcesWeb Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
let MuddyViperIPs = dynamic(["3.95.7.142","35.175.224.64","51.16.209.105","62.106.66.112","157.20.182.45","161.35.172.55","167.99.224.13","194.11.246.78","194.11.246.101","206.71.149.51","212.232.22.136"]);
let MuddyViperDomains = dynamic(["processplanet.org","api.tikavodot.co.il","magicallyday.com"]);
let MuddyViperUriTokens = dynamic(["/adad","/aq36","/mq65","/oi32","/dadw","/dadwqa","/rq13"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("ConnectionSuccess","HttpConnectionInspected","ConnectionAttempt")
| where RemoteIP in (MuddyViperIPs)
   or RemoteUrl has_any (MuddyViperDomains)
   or RemoteUrl has_any (MuddyViperUriTokens)
   or AdditionalFields has_cs "A WinHTTP Example Program/1.0"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msedge.exe","chrome.exe","firefox.exe","iexplore.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, AdditionalFields
| order by Timestamp desc
let MuddyViperIPs = dynamic(["3.95.7.142","35.175.224.64","51.16.209.105","62.106.66.112","157.20.182.45","161.35.172.55","167.99.224.13","194.11.246.78","194.11.246.101","206.71.149.51","212.232.22.136"]);
let MuddyViperDomains = dynamic(["processplanet.org","api.tikavodot.co.il","magicallyday.com"]);
let MuddyViperUriTokens = dynamic(["/adad","/aq36","/mq65","/oi32","/dadw","/dadwqa","/rq13"]);
let ProxyHits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DestinationHostName has_any (MuddyViperDomains)
         or DestinationIP in (MuddyViperIPs)
         or RequestURL has_any (MuddyViperUriTokens)
         or RequestClientApplication has_cs "A WinHTTP Example Program/1.0"
    | project TimeGenerated, Source = SourceIP, User = SourceUserName, Computer = SourceHostName,
              DestinationIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication,
              Signal = "Proxy";
let WebHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DstHostname has_any (MuddyViperDomains)
         or DstIpAddr in (MuddyViperIPs)
         or Url has_any (MuddyViperUriTokens)
         or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has_cs "A WinHTTP Example Program/1.0"
    | project TimeGenerated, Source = SrcIpAddr, User = SrcUsername, Computer = SrcHostname,
              DestinationIP = DstIpAddr, DestinationHostName = DstHostname,
              RequestURL = Url, RequestClientApplication = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent,
              Signal = "ASIM";
let DnsHits = DnsEvents
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Name has_any (MuddyViperDomains)
    | project TimeGenerated, Source = ClientIP, User = "", Computer = Computer,
              DestinationIP = "", DestinationHostName = Name,
              RequestURL = "", RequestClientApplication = "", Signal = "DNS";
union ProxyHits, WebHits, DnsHits
| order by TimeGenerated desc

[LLM] MuddyWater CE-Notes / LP-Notes / Blub stealer staging-file writes Actions on Objectives High
Detects creation of the distinctive staging files used by MuddyWater's CE-Notes and LP-Notes credential stealers and the Blub browser-data stealer: ce-notes.txt and lp-notes.txt under C:\Users\Public\Downloads\, and the system2.dll / system.dll drop locations under C:\, C:\Intel\, and C:\Users\Public\Downloads\. Rationale: ESET documents these exact staging filenames and paths verbatim for CE-Notes (ce-notes.txt, system2.dll), LP-Notes (lp-notes.txt), and their secondary drop locations (C:\system2.dll, C:\Intel\system.dll). C:\Users\Public\Downloads is a non-standard staging path for legitimate Windows software, making file-name + path combinations very low FP. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/muddywater-snakes-riverbank/ • https://securityaffairs.com/185244/apt/muddywater-strikes-israel-with-advanced-muddyviper-malware.html
ATT&CKT1555.003 T1074.001 T1003.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where (FolderPath =~ @"C:\Users\Public\Downloads" and FileName in~ ("ce-notes.txt","lp-notes.txt","system2.dll"))
   or (FolderPath =~ @"C:\" and FileName =~ "system2.dll")
   or (FolderPath =~ @"C:\Intel" and FileName =~ "system.dll")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msmpeng.exe","mssense.exe","sense.exe","explorer.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA1, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where (TargetFilePath =~ @"C:\Users\Public\Downloads\ce-notes.txt")
   or (TargetFilePath =~ @"C:\Users\Public\Downloads\lp-notes.txt")
   or (TargetFilePath =~ @"C:\Users\Public\Downloads\system2.dll")
   or (TargetFilePath =~ @"C:\system2.dll")
   or (TargetFilePath =~ @"C:\Intel\system.dll")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("explorer.exe","msiexec.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFileSHA1, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

[LLM] Non-browser process reading Chrome/Edge/Opera Login Data or Local State Actions on Objectives High
Detects MuddyWater's Blub and CE-Notes browser-data stealers by hunting for non-browser processes opening the Chrome/Edge/Opera 'Login Data' SQLite store or the 'Local State' file (which holds the DPAPI-protected master encryption key). Includes the campaign-specific Blub behaviour of terminating chrome.exe immediately before reading. Rationale: Directly maps ESET's documented browser-data theft paths (Chrome/Edge/Opera Local State + per-profile Login Data, Firefox logins.json/key4.db). The taskhostw.exe pivot in the Suspicious column captures LP-Notes' ImpersonateLoggedOnUser masquerade — taskhostw.exe legitimately lives in C:\Windows\System32 and never reads Chrome credential stores, so any deviation is high signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/muddywater-snakes-riverbank/ • https://securityaffairs.com/185244/apt/muddywater-strikes-israel-with-advanced-muddyviper-malware.html
ATT&CKT1555.003 T1140 T1134.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileCreated" or ActionType == "FileOpened" or ActionType == "FileRenamed"
| where (FolderPath has @"\Google\Chrome\User Data" and (FileName =~ "Local State" or FileName =~ "Login Data"))
     or (FolderPath has @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data" and (FileName =~ "Local State" or FileName =~ "Login Data"))
     or (FolderPath has @"\Opera Software\Opera Stable" and FileName =~ "Local State")
     or (FolderPath has @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles" and FileName in~ ("logins.json","key4.db"))
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","opera.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","vivaldi.exe","msmpeng.exe","mssense.exe","sense.exe","explorer.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| extend Suspicious =
      case(InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "taskhostw.exe" and InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\System32", "LP-Notes_taskhostw_impersonation",
           InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\Users\Public\Downloads\", "PublicDownloads_stealer",
           "non-browser-secret-read")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, Suspicious,
          FileName, FolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessSHA1
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileAccessed","FileRenamed")
| where (TargetFilePath has @"\Google\Chrome\User Data" and (TargetFileName =~ "Local State" or TargetFileName =~ "Login Data"))
     or (TargetFilePath has @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data" and (TargetFileName =~ "Local State" or TargetFileName =~ "Login Data"))
     or (TargetFilePath has @"\Opera Software\Opera Stable" and TargetFileName =~ "Local State")
     or (TargetFilePath has @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles" and TargetFileName in~ ("logins.json","key4.db"))
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","opera.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","vivaldi.exe","explorer.exe")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          TargetFileSHA1, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1555.003
  - attack.credential_access

  category: file_access
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_target:
    TargetFilename|endswith:
      - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\Local State'
      - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\Local State'

      - '\logins.json'

      - '\brave.exe'
      - '\vivaldi.exe'

level: high

#event_simpleName=FileOpenInfo
| TargetFileName=/\\(Google\\Chrome\\User Data\\(Local State|.+\\Login Data)|Microsoft\\Edge\\User Data\\(Local State|.+\\Login Data)|Opera Software\\Opera Stable\\Local State|Mozilla\\Firefox\\Profiles\\.+\\(logins\.json|key4\.db))$/i

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-48633")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-48633")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2025-48633")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2025-48633")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-26829")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-26829")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Article-specific bespoke detection — Snyk Log Sniffer: AI-Powered Audit Log Insights for Security Leaders

DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("node.js"))

          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

[LLM] SHA1-Hulud worm payload execution via npm preinstall (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) Installation High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud worm's signature preinstall payload — node spawned by npm during package install executing setup_bun.js or bun_environment.js. This is the foothold the worm needs before pivoting to credential theft and GitHub Actions self-hosted runner registration. Rationale: setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js are the canonical SHA1-Hulud worm payload filenames named in Wiz, Datadog, Trend Micro and Palo Alto Unit 42 write-ups; pairing them with npm/node parent process is high-fidelity supply-chain compromise signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/k/shai-hulud-npm-worm.html
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents

| where (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npm","node","yarn","pnpm")
         or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","preinstall"))
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("npm","node","yarn","pnpm")
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","preinstall")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("setup_bun.js","bun_environment.js")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: SHA1-Hulud npm Worm Preinstall Payload Execution
id: 9d3a5b1c-4e2a-4f6a-9c1e-7b3a1e9d4f5a
description: Detects the SHA1-Hulud npm supply chain worm's preinstall payload (setup_bun.js / bun_environment.js) spawned from npm/node lifecycle hooks.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/sha1-hulud-npm-supply-chain-incident/
  - https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack

date: 2026/05/21

  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\yarn.exe'
      - '\pnpm.exe'
  selection_payload:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'setup_bun.js'
      - 'bun_environment.js'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_payload
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR linux.auditd) (@ParentImage:(*\\npm.exe OR *\\node.exe OR *\\yarn.exe OR *\\pnpm.exe) OR @process.parent.name:(npm OR node OR yarn OR pnpm)) (@CommandLine:(*setup_bun.js* OR *bun_environment.js*) OR @process.command_line:(*setup_bun.js* OR *bun_environment.js*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(npm|node|yarn|pnpm)(\.exe)?$/i
| regex("(setup_bun\.js|bun_environment\.js)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, SHA256HashData], function=count())

[LLM] TruffleHog secret-scanner execution on developer / CI host (SHA1-Hulud credential harvest) Actions on Objectives High
Hunts execution of the TruffleHog binary on workstations and CI runners — the SHA1-Hulud worm bundles TruffleHog to harvest GitHub PATs, npm tokens and cloud keys from disk before exfiltration. TruffleHog is legitimate on security-team boxes but anomalous everywhere else. Rationale: TruffleHog usage by the SHA1-Hulud worm is corroborated by Wiz, Trend Micro and Datadog reports; scoping the parent to node/npm/bun/shell distinguishes worm activity from legitimate AppSec use. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/shai-hulud-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/k/shai-hulud-npm-worm.html

[LLM] Outbound exfiltration to webhook.site from npm / node / bun process tree Command & Control High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud exfiltration channel — POST requests to webhook.site originated by node/npm/bun/yarn processes. webhook.site is the worm's canonical drop point for stolen GitHub PATs and cloud credentials. [LLM] Cloud metadata service (IMDS) access from npm / node child process Actions on Objectives High
Hunts SHA1-Hulud's cloud-credential harvest stage — node/npm-spawned processes hitting AWS 169.254.169.254 IMDS, Azure 169.254.169.254 metadata, or GCP metadata.google.internal. Legitimate apps reach IMDS, but never via a freshly-installed npm package's preinstall context. Rationale: Cloud credential harvest from AWS/Azure/GCP IMDS is the article's named escalation path and corroborated by Wiz + Unit 42; the 169.254.169.254 link-local + node-family parent is a high-signal combination because legitimate apps that hit IMDS rarely come from a fresh npm install context. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1552.005 T1078.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","bun.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","node","npm","bun","curl.exe","wget.exe")

[LLM] Malicious '.github/workflows/discussion.yaml' workflow file created by npm/node Installation High
Detects SHA1-Hulud's GitHub Actions persistence: the worm writes a discussion.yaml workflow file under .github/workflows/ inside cloned/checked-out repositories to inject self-hosted runner registration and exfiltration steps that propagate to downstream pipelines. Rationale: discussion.yaml workflow file injection is the documented SHA1-Hulud GitHub Actions persistence mechanism (Wiz, Datadog); requiring an npm/node parent removes legitimate developer commits adding a workflow named discussion. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.datadoghq.com/blog/shai-hulud-supply-chain-attack/

[LLM] SHA1-Hulud wiper: mass deletion of user home directory by npm/node descendant Actions on Objectives High
Hunts the SHA1-Hulud destructive-fallback wiper — when credential harvest fails, the worm deletes the developer's home directory. Detects high-volume file delete events under %USERPROFILE% / /home/* whose initiating process traces back to node/npm/bun. Rationale: The wiper / home-directory-deletion destructive fallback is explicitly named in the article's Nov 24 3:30pm update and corroborated by Wiz and Unit 42; a node/npm/bun-descended process performing >200 deletes under a user home in 5 minutes is anomalous outside `rm -rf node_modules` (which doesn't recurse through ~ ). Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-second-coming-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1485 T1561.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileDeleted"
| where FolderPath startswith @"C:\Users\" or FolderPath startswith "/home/" or FolderPath startswith "/Users/" or FolderPath startswith "/root/"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","bun.exe","node","npm","bun","rm","sh","bash","zsh","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| summarize DeleteCount = count(),
            SamplePaths = make_set(FolderPath, 10),
            FirstDelete = min(Timestamp),
            LastDelete = max(Timestamp),
            DistinctTopLevelDirs = dcount(tostring(split(FolderPath, @"\")[2]))
            by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, bin(Timestamp, 5m)
| where DeleteCount > 200
| order by FirstDelete desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType == "FileDeleted"

| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","npm","bun","rm","sh","bash","zsh","powershell","pwsh")
| summarize DeleteCount = count(),
            FirstDelete = min(TimeGenerated),
            LastDelete = max(TimeGenerated)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| where DeleteCount > 200
| order by FirstDelete desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url

| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase

    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation

            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Critical

Shai-Hulud: Self-Replicating Worm Compromises 500+ NPM Packages

StepSecurity
2025-11-23 10 use cases 4 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

The Shai-Hulud worm has infected over 500 NPM packages including @ctrl/tinycolor in an unprecedented self-propagating supply chain attack. The malware harvests AWS/GCP/Azure credentials using TruffleHog, establishes persistence through GitHub Actions backdoors, and automatically spreads to other maintainer packages - marking the first successful worm attack in the NPM ecosystem.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Shai-Hulud: Self-Replicating Worm Compromises 500+ NPM Packages
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Shai-Hulud bundle.js dropped on disk (SHA256 + filename hunt)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound exfiltration to Shai-Hulud webhook.site/bb8ca5f6 C2 endpoint · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] TruffleHog spawned by node/npm as postinstall — Shai-Hulud credential sweep · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
[LLM] Outbound exfiltration to Shai-Hulud webhook.site/bb8ca5f6 C2 endpoint Command & Control High
Hunts any host (developer workstation or CI runner) that contacts the specific webhook.site UUID path used by the Shai-Hulud NPM worm for credential exfiltration. The full path is a unique, attacker-controlled drop-point — any contact is a high-confidence indicator of a compromised package install or a backdoored shai-hulud-workflow.yml execution. Rationale: The webhook.site UUID path bb8ca5f6-4175-45d2-b042-fc9ebb8170b7 is unique to this campaign and corroborated by Wiz, Unit42, Sysdig, and ReversingLabs as the sole network IOC. Any hit is high-confidence compromise either by direct npm install of a poisoned package or by the planted shai-hulud-workflow.yml in a victim repo running on a GitHub Actions runner with egress visibility. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm

[LLM] TruffleHog spawned by node/npm as postinstall — Shai-Hulud credential sweep Actions on Objectives High
Detects the worm's defining behaviour: a Node.js/npm/yarn/pnpm process spawning trufflehog with 'filesystem' and root path arguments (e.g. `trufflehog filesystem / --json`) on a Linux or macOS developer workstation or CI runner. Legitimate developers rarely have npm install fork trufflehog at the filesystem root. Rationale: The article explicitly identifies `child_process.exec('trufflehog filesystem / --json')` invoked by Module 94913 of the Webpack bundle during npm install. The parent-child constraint (node/npm/yarn → trufflehog with `filesystem` arg) is virtually unseen in benign developer activity. Corroborated by Sysdig and Semgrep's Shai-Hulud 2.0 analyses showing the trufflehog binary as the canonical harvesting tool across both worm generations. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/digging-for-secrets-sha1-hulud-the-second-coming-of-the-npm-worm/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1195.002 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where DeviceName !in~ ("") // optional scope
| where FileName =~ "trufflehog"
   or InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "trufflehog"
   or ProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "filesystem"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--json"," / "," /--","filesystem /")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","bun","sh","bash","zsh")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","npx ","postinstall")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessName has "trufflehog"
   or ActingProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "filesystem"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("--json","filesystem /"," / ")
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","bun","sh","bash","zsh")
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install","postinstall")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

[LLM] Shai-Hulud bundle.js dropped on disk (SHA256 + filename hunt) Installation High
Catches the known Shai-Hulud Webpack payload by its SHA256 (46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09) or by the file `shai-hulud-workflow.yml` landing in a `.github/workflows/` path on a developer workstation, indicating either an infected package was installed or the worm wrote its persistence workflow into a local repo clone. Rationale: The bundle.js SHA256 (46faab...8f09) is published by StepSecurity and re-confirmed across Wiz, ReversingLabs, and Unit42 reports as the canonical first-wave payload. The filename `shai-hulud-workflow.yml` under `.github/workflows/` is the persistence artefact placed in every victim repository (text + bash script in the article body) and is uniquely tied to this campaign. Both indicators are static IOC matches with effectively zero benign overlap. Cross-checked against: • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://cybersecurefox.com/en/shai-hulud-npm-worm-github-actions-supply-chain/

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime

        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

// Article-specific bespoke detection — Shai-Hulud: Self-Replicating Worm Compromises 500+ NPM Packages
